    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. Smith, I... I didn't really want to come here today, to be honest. It's my lieutenant. He, uh, he's the one who insisted I come in. See, I'm an EMT, and at the station, we have this whole system for the rigs, right? The jump bags, the O2, the airway bags... it's all supposed to be a certain way. But lately, I've been seeing how it really needs to be. So, I was in the bay, and I was moving the airway bags. Not just moving them, but putting them into this specific pattern. It's... it's hard to explain to someone who isn't there, but the pattern is right. It's the only way it works. 

And while I was doing it, I had to keep repeating the dispatch numbers. Out loud, you know? Like, over and over, because the numbers tie into the pattern. If you don't say the numbers, the pattern doesn't set. So I'm doing this, making sure it's perfect, and my lieutenant comes over and tells me to stop. He's telling me to step away from the rig. But I couldn't. I just couldn't. I tried to tell him, but he wouldn't listen. If I had stopped, if I had just stepped away and broken that sequence... I don't know, it just felt like the whole bay, the whole station, would just go bad. Like something terrible would happen if the sequence wasn't finished. So I wouldn't move. And now... now I'm here. Because he didn't understand the pattern.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Excessive? No, I... why would I think it's excessive? That doesn't make any sense. It's not a... it's not some imagination thing, Dr. Smith. It's a real, physical certainty. Even when I'm not right there in the bay, even if I'm just sitting in the break room drinking coffee, or driving my car, I know exactly what the stakes are. 

Because you have to understand how the dispatch system works. The numbers they give us, they aren't just random digits, they are tied to the calls, to the reality of what's happening out on the streets. And the equipment in the rig—the O2, the airway bags, the trauma dressings—they have to match that reality. So if I don't finish the pattern, the alignment is off. It's like... it's like leaving a door wide open in a hurricane. You wouldn't sit there in your living room while the wind is tearing your roof off and think, "Gee, maybe my fear of the storm is excessive." You'd know the danger is right there. It's undeniable. 

It's the exact same thing with the rigs. The danger is absolutely real, one hundred percent, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. If the sequence is broken, the bad thing is already happening. It's already bleeding into the station. That's why I couldn't step away when the lieutenant told me to. He was basically asking me to just let the hurricane in. He just sees a guy moving bags around, he doesn't see how the numbers lock it all down and keep us safe. So, no, I never doubt it. I never think it's not true. I honestly just sit here worrying that because he made me stop, because I'm here in this office right now instead of finishing the sequence, something terrible is probably happening to my crew on shift today.

Well, I haven't been hearing voices or anything like that, if that's what you mean. The station is loud enough as it is, you know, with the tones dropping and the radios constantly squawking. I don't need to hear things that aren't there. But... well, since you mention other things being connected, there is this whole other situation going on at the station. And it's not just a feeling, it's something I know for a fact is happening, even though they try to play it off as nothing.

See, we have these cameras in the bays and around the station. Supposedly for security, right? To watch the rigs, make sure nobody wanders in off the street and messes with the narcotics box or steals our gear. That's what they tell us. But I know for a fact that the injury review people—the ones downtown in the admin building—and some of my own supervisors are using those cameras to watch me. Specifically me. They sit there and monitor what I'm doing, how I'm moving the bags, how I'm running my checks. 

And it doesn't stop there. It's the paperwork, too. Every time we run a call, we have to fill out these electronic patient care reports, the ePCRs. You have to document everything—the vitals, the interventions, the triage notes. It's a whole process, and I'm very meticulous about it. I have to be. But lately, I've been going back into the system to check my reports, and things are changed. My triage notes, the narratives I write... they're altered. Just slightly, but enough to make it look like I'm making mistakes. Like I'm being careless or unsafe on scene. 

I even confronted one of the supervisors about it. I brought him the printouts, showed him the discrepancies from what I originally typed. And you know what he said? He just brushed it off. He told me it was just "routine paperwork audits" and that the system sometimes auto-corrects or updates formatting. Formatting! You don't auto-correct a GCS score or a trauma criteria check! They're doing it on purpose, Dr. Smith. They're watching me through those lenses, tracking my movements, and then going into the secure servers to change my notes. They want to build a case against me to prove I'm unsafe, probably because they know I understand the patterns and they don't. They want me out of the way before I can get the rigs properly aligned.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad? Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. 

And as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. 

And on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. 

Honestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.

It's the only way it works. \n\nAnd while I was doing it, I had to keep repeating the dispatch numbers. Out loud, you know? Like, over and over, because the numbers tie into the pattern. If you don't say the numbers, the pattern doesn't set. So I'm doing this, making sure it's perfect, and my lieutenant comes over and tells me to stop. He's telling me to step away from the rig. But I couldn't. I just couldn't. I tried to tell him, but he wouldn't listen. If I had stopped, if I had just stepped away and broken that sequence... I don't know, it just felt like the whole bay, the whole station, would just go bad. Like something terrible would happen if the sequence wasn't finished. So I wouldn't move. And now... now I'm here. Because he didn't understand the pattern.\n\nDoctor: When you're not in the middle of doing the pattern, do you ever think to yourself that the fear something bad will happen if you don't finish it might be excessive or not really true, or do you feel certain that it's a real danger no matter what?\n\nPatient: Excessive? No, I... why would I think it's excessive? That doesn't make any sense. It's not a... it's not some imagination thing, Dr. Smith. It's a real, physical certainty. Even when I'm not right there in the bay, even if I'm just sitting in the break room drinking coffee, or driving my car, I know exactly what the stakes are. \n\nBecause you have to understand how the dispatch system works. The numbers they give us, they aren't just random digits, they are tied to the calls, to the reality of what's happening out on the streets. And the equipment in the rig—the O2, the airway bags, the trauma dressings—they have to match that reality. So if I don't finish the pattern, the alignment is off. It's like... it's like leaving a door wide open in a hurricane. You wouldn't sit there in your living room while the wind is tearing your roof off and think, \"Gee, maybe my fear of the storm is excessive.\" You'd know the danger is right there. It's undeniable. \n\nIt's the exact same thing with the rigs. The danger is absolutely real, one hundred percent, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. If the sequence is broken, the bad thing is already happening. It's already bleeding into the station. That's why I couldn't step away when the lieutenant told me to. He was basically asking me to just let the hurricane in. He just sees a guy moving bags around, he doesn't see how the numbers lock it all down and keep us safe. So, no, I never doubt it. I never think it's not true. I honestly just sit here worrying that because he made me stop, because I'm here in this office right now instead of finishing the sequence, something terrible is probably happening to my crew on shift today.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this concern about the equipment patterns and numbers, have you noticed anything else unusual lately—like hearing things other people don't hear, or feeling that other unrelated events are also connected or significant in a special way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't been hearing voices or anything like that, if that's what you mean. The station is loud enough as it is, you know, with the tones dropping and the radios constantly squawking. I don't need to hear things that aren't there. But... well, since you mention other things being connected, there is this whole other situation going on at the station. And it's not just a feeling, it's something I know for a fact is happening, even though they try to play it off as nothing.\n\nSee, we have these cameras in the bays and around the station. Supposedly for security, right? To watch the rigs, make sure nobody wanders in off the street and messes with the narcotics box or steals our gear. That's what they tell us. But I know for a fact that the injury review people—the ones downtown in the admin building—and some of my own supervisors are using those cameras to watch me. Specifically me. They sit there and monitor what I'm doing, how I'm moving the bags, how I'm running my checks. \n\nAnd it doesn't stop there. It's the paperwork, too. Every time we run a call, we have to fill out these electronic patient care reports, the ePCRs. You have to document everything—the vitals, the interventions, the triage notes. It's a whole process, and I'm very meticulous about it. I have to be. But lately, I've been going back into the system to check my reports, and things are changed. My triage notes, the narratives I write... they're altered. Just slightly, but enough to make it look like I'm making mistakes. Like I'm being careless or unsafe on scene. \n\nI even confronted one of the supervisors about it. I brought him the printouts, showed him the discrepancies from what I originally typed. And you know what he said? He just brushed it off. He told me it was just \"routine paperwork audits\" and that the system sometimes auto-corrects or updates formatting. Formatting! You don't auto-correct a GCS score or a trauma criteria check! They're doing it on purpose, Dr. Smith. They're watching me through those lenses, tracking my movements, and then going into the secure servers to change my notes. They want to build a case against me to prove I'm unsafe, probably because they know I understand the patterns and they don't. They want me out of the way before I can get the rigs properly aligned.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several weeks, have you also noticed any prolonged periods of feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or low on energy, or conversely periods of feeling extremely elated, energetic, or needing little sleep?\n\nPatient: Sad? Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. \n\nAnd as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. \n\nAnd on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F20.9", "F22"], "note": "Patient denies any depressive or manic mood symptoms, ruling out F25.9 (schizoaffective disorder). He also firmly denies any excessiveness or doubt about his beliefs regarding the patterns/numbers, indicating fixed delusional conviction rather than obsessions with intact insight, which argues against F42 (OCD). The combination of persecutory delusions (surveillance, altered records) and magical-thinking delusions about number/pattern alignment now points toward a primary psychotic disorder—either schizophrenia (if functional decline and disorganization are prominent) or delusional disorder (if delusions remain the sole psychotic feature without broader disorganization).", "is_final": false}

You wouldn't sit there in your living room while the wind is tearing your roof off and think, \"Gee, maybe my fear of the storm is excessive.\" You'd know the danger is right there. It's undeniable. \n\nIt's the exact same thing with the rigs. The danger is absolutely real, one hundred percent, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. If the sequence is broken, the bad thing is already happening. It's already bleeding into the station. That's why I couldn't step away when the lieutenant told me to. He was basically asking me to just let the hurricane in. He just sees a guy moving bags around, he doesn't see how the numbers lock it all down and keep us safe. So, no, I never doubt it. I never think it's not true. I honestly just sit here worrying that because he made me stop, because I'm here in this office right now instead of finishing the sequence, something terrible is probably happening to my crew on shift today.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this concern about the equipment patterns and numbers, have you noticed anything else unusual lately—like hearing things other people don't hear, or feeling that other unrelated events are also connected or significant in a special way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't been hearing voices or anything like that, if that's what you mean. The station is loud enough as it is, you know, with the tones dropping and the radios constantly squawking. I don't need to hear things that aren't there. But... well, since you mention other things being connected, there is this whole other situation going on at the station. And it's not just a feeling, it's something I know for a fact is happening, even though they try to play it off as nothing.\n\nSee, we have these cameras in the bays and around the station. Supposedly for security, right? To watch the rigs, make sure nobody wanders in off the street and messes with the narcotics box or steals our gear. That's what they tell us. But I know for a fact that the injury review people—the ones downtown in the admin building—and some of my own supervisors are using those cameras to watch me. Specifically me. They sit there and monitor what I'm doing, how I'm moving the bags, how I'm running my checks. \n\nAnd it doesn't stop there. It's the paperwork, too. Every time we run a call, we have to fill out these electronic patient care reports, the ePCRs. You have to document everything—the vitals, the interventions, the triage notes. It's a whole process, and I'm very meticulous about it. I have to be. But lately, I've been going back into the system to check my reports, and things are changed. My triage notes, the narratives I write... they're altered. Just slightly, but enough to make it look like I'm making mistakes. Like I'm being careless or unsafe on scene. \n\nI even confronted one of the supervisors about it. I brought him the printouts, showed him the discrepancies from what I originally typed. And you know what he said? He just brushed it off. He told me it was just \"routine paperwork audits\" and that the system sometimes auto-corrects or updates formatting. Formatting! You don't auto-correct a GCS score or a trauma criteria check! They're doing it on purpose, Dr. Smith. They're watching me through those lenses, tracking my movements, and then going into the secure servers to change my notes. They want to build a case against me to prove I'm unsafe, probably because they know I understand the patterns and they don't. They want me out of the way before I can get the rigs properly aligned.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several weeks, have you also noticed any prolonged periods of feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or low on energy, or conversely periods of feeling extremely elated, energetic, or needing little sleep?\n\nPatient: Sad? Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. \n\nAnd as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. \n\nAnd on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long has this specific concern about the equipment patterns, dispatch numbers, and being watched/altered records been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\nmanifestation: \"The camera and paperwork stuff, and the station behavior that got everyone alarmed, have been going on for about the last two months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Months. Definitely months. Well, about two months, to be exact. I mean, it is not like they just flipped a switch overnight, you know? But it was right around... yeah, about two months ago. That is when I first started really piecing it all together and seeing the scope of what they were doing. 

I remember because that is when I first noticed the lens on the main bay camera physically tracking me. The little indicator light would stay on way longer than it was supposed to after I walked past the rigs, just watching me arrange the trauma bags. And the paperwork stuff, the ePCRs... that started right around the exact same time. I can trace it back to this one specific shift about eight weeks ago. We had a really heavy rotation, and I spent an extra forty-five minutes at the end of my shift making sure every single triage note and trauma criteria check was perfectly, absolutely perfectly documented. And then a few days later, I go back into the secure server, and the formatting is changed. The notes are shifted just enough to look sloppy. 

So, yeah, the camera and the paperwork stuff has been going on for about the last two months. And of course, that is exactly when the rest of the station started getting all worked up about me. The lieutenant pulling me into the office, the whispers in the day room... all that station behavior that got everyone so alarmed. They act like I am the one doing something wrong, getting all panicked and alarmed just because I am taking the time to make sure the equipment is properly aligned with the dispatch codes. 

Two solid months of me just trying to do my job and keep the crew safe, and two months of them watching my every move, changing my official medical notes, and acting like I am the problem. It is just... it takes a massive amount of focus to deal with that kind of sabotage every single shift for eight straight weeks, Dr. Smith. If they would just open their eyes and look at how the numbers correlate to the streets, they would understand, but instead they have just spent the last two months trying to build this ridiculous case to push me out.

You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: How long has this specific concern about the equipment patterns, dispatch numbers, and being watched/altered records been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: Months. Definitely months. Well, about two months, to be exact. I mean, it is not like they just flipped a switch overnight, you know? But it was right around... yeah, about two months ago. That is when I first started really piecing it all together and seeing the scope of what they were doing. \n\nI remember because that is when I first noticed the lens on the main bay camera physically tracking me. The little indicator light would stay on way longer than it was supposed to after I walked past the rigs, just watching me arrange the trauma bags. And the paperwork stuff, the ePCRs... that started right around the exact same time. I can trace it back to this one specific shift about eight weeks ago. We had a really heavy rotation, and I spent an extra forty-five minutes at the end of my shift making sure every single triage note and trauma criteria check was perfectly, absolutely perfectly documented. And then a few days later, I go back into the secure server, and the formatting is changed. The notes are shifted just enough to look sloppy. \n\nSo, yeah, the camera and the paperwork stuff has been going on for about the last two months. And of course, that is exactly when the rest of the station started getting all worked up about me. The lieutenant pulling me into the office, the whispers in the day room... all that station behavior that got everyone so alarmed. They act like I am the one doing something wrong, getting all panicked and alarmed just because I am taking the time to make sure the equipment is properly aligned with the dispatch codes. \n\nTwo solid months of me just trying to do my job and keep the crew safe, and two months of them watching my every move, changing my official medical notes, and acting like I am the problem. It is just... it takes a massive amount of focus to deal with that kind of sabotage every single shift for eight straight weeks, Dr. Smith. If they would just open their eyes and look at how the numbers correlate to the streets, they would understand, but instead they have just spent the last two months trying to build this ridiculous case to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these concerns about the equipment, cameras, and paperwork, how have things been going in other areas of your life lately—like your relationships with family or friends, your ability to relax or enjoy hobbies, and your day-to-day self-care?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of the station... there isn't really much of an 'outside' anymore, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I mean, when my shift ends and I finally leave that environment, I go back to my apartment and... I just sit there. \n\nIt's hard to explain, but it's like the second I walk through my front door, everything just stops. Most days I just sit in my apartment, usually right on the edge of the couch, sometimes without even taking my boots off right away. I don't turn on the TV, I don't put on any music. I just sit. I look over, and there's just trash piling up in the corner of the room. I can see it, I know I need to gather it up and walk it down to the dumpster behind the complex, but I just... I don't. I just stare at it. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my kitchen. I used to be the guy who cooked all the time, you know? You have to stay healthy in EMS, so I\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these past two months when the camera and paperwork concerns became clear, did you notice any earlier changes—even subtle ones, like being more withdrawn, less motivated, or preoccupied with rules and patterns—that started earlier, say three to six months ago?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This all started after I hurt my shoulder and ribs during that ambulance transfer about eight months ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if we're really going back... if we're really looking at the timeline of when things started shifting, it wasn't just out of nowhere. It actually goes back a lot further than two months. It started... gosh, it must have been about eight months ago. Yeah, eight months. 

See, there was this transfer we were doing. It was supposed to be a routine bariatric transport, nothing we haven't done a hundred times, but the gurney caught on the threshold of the ER bay doors. It just jammed, and the whole weight shifted, and I had to throw my entire body weight into it to keep the patient from going over. I ended up wrenching my shoulder pretty bad, and I cracked a couple of ribs on the side rail. 

It was excruciating, Dr. Smith. Just breathing hurt. I had to take time off, obviously, because you can't lift a backboard with busted ribs and a torn rotator cuff. And being stuck at home... that's when things started getting quiet. Too quiet. I was just sitting there, day after day, waiting to heal, and I started having all this time to think. I wasn't out on the rigs, I wasn't running calls, I was just... detached. 

I guess you could say I was withdrawn, but it wasn't intentional, it was just that I couldn't physically do anything. And when I finally did get cleared for light duty and then back to full duty, I just... I wasn't the same. I couldn't just brush off the sloppy work anymore. I started noticing how careless everyone was being with the equipment, how the protocols weren't being followed to the letter. I started realizing that if I didn't pay attention to the patterns, if I didn't make sure the rules were strictly enforced, someone else was going to get hurt, or worse. 

So, yes, the intense focus on the patterns, the need to have everything perfectly aligned... that really started building up after the injury, about eight months ago. I had to make sure the environment was controlled, because I learned the hard way what happens when things slip out of control. It just took a while for it to escalate to what's happening now with the cameras and the records, but the foundation of it, the real beginning... that was the shoulder and rib injury. That's when I really started to see how fragile the whole system is.

Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. \n\nAnd as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. \n\nAnd on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: How long has this specific concern about the equipment patterns, dispatch numbers, and being watched/altered records been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: Months. Definitely months. Well, about two months, to be exact. I mean, it is not like they just flipped a switch overnight, you know? But it was right around... yeah, about two months ago. That is when I first started really piecing it all together and seeing the scope of what they were doing. \n\nI remember because that is when I first noticed the lens on the main bay camera physically tracking me. The little indicator light would stay on way longer than it was supposed to after I walked past the rigs, just watching me arrange the trauma bags. We had a really heavy rotation, and I spent an extra forty-five minutes at the end of my shift making sure every single triage note and trauma criteria check was perfectly, absolutely perfectly documented. And then a few days later, I go back into the secure server, and the formatting is changed. The notes are shifted just enough to look sloppy. \n\nSo, yeah, the camera and the paperwork stuff has been going on for about the last two months. And of course, that is exactly when the rest of the station started getting all worked up about me. The lieutenant pulling me into the office, the whispers in the day room... all that station behavior that got everyone so alarmed. They act like I am the one doing something wrong, getting all panicked and alarmed just because I am taking the time to make sure the equipment is properly aligned with the dispatch codes. \n\nTwo solid months of me just trying to do my job and keep the crew safe, and two months of them watching my every move, changing my official medical notes, and acting like I am the problem. It is just... it takes a massive amount of focus to deal with that kind of sabotage every single shift for eight straight weeks, Dr. Smith. If they would just open their eyes and look at how the numbers correlate to the streets, they would understand, but instead they have just spent the last two months trying to build this ridiculous case to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these concerns about the equipment, cameras, and paperwork, how have things been going in other areas of your life lately—like your relationships with family or friends, your ability to relax or enjoy hobbies, and your day-to-day self-care?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of the station... there isn't really much of an 'outside' anymore, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I ended up wrenching my shoulder pretty bad, and I cracked a couple of ribs on the side rail. \n\nIt was excruciating, Dr. Smith. Just breathing hurt. No... no, there weren't any stretches where I felt back to normal. Not really. It's been pretty much a continuous, straight line ever since I got hurt eight months ago. I mean, I kept thinking maybe once the ribs healed and the shoulder stopped throbbing I’d just naturally bounce back to my old routine, but... well, like I was trying to explain before, most days I just sit in my apartment. I literally just sit there. And it's been exactly like that the whole time. 

I just sit there with the trash piling up in the corner, and... and the kitchen, right, I was going to tell you about the kitchen earlier. The food is still in the fridge. I’ll go to the grocery store on auto-pilot, I’ll buy chicken and vegetables and rice because you have to eat right to do this job, but then I just put it away and leave it in the drawers until it rots. I don't cook like I used to. I used to meal prep every single Sunday, I had all these glass containers perfectly stacked and portioned out for the week... but now, I just can't get myself to start anything. It's too much. 

And it’s not just the cooking or the apartment. It’s everything else, too. I used to play pickup basketball with some of the guys from the other stations. Tuesday and Thursday mornings, without fail, we’d be out there at the community center running full-court games. I was always the one organizing it, sending the group texts, making sure we had enough guys for five-on-five. But since the injury? I haven't called the guys for basketball once. Not a single time in eight solid months. I see their names in my phone, I know I should probably reach out or at least go shoot around, but... I just can't get myself to care. I really can't. 

It's like the starter motor in my head is just totally disconnected. I sit there on the edge of the couch, I look at the trash bags in the corner, I know the food is going bad in the fridge, I know the guys are probably playing without me, and I just... I don't care. I can't make myself care enough to just stand up and do any of it. So, yeah, to answer your question, it hasn't stopped or gotten better. It's just been this continuous, heavy feeling of not being able to start anything, just sitting there day after day.

The jump bags, the O2, the airway bags... it's all supposed to be a certain way. But lately, I've been seeing how it really needs to be. So, I was in the bay, and I was moving the airway bags. Not just moving them, but putting them into this specific pattern. It's... it's hard to explain to someone who isn't there, but the pattern is right. It's the only way it works. \n\nAnd while I was doing it, I had to keep repeating the dispatch numbers. Out loud, you know? Like, over and over, because the numbers tie into the pattern. If you don't say the numbers, the pattern doesn't set. So I'm doing this, making sure it's perfect, and my lieutenant comes over and tells me to stop. He's telling me to step away from the rig. But I couldn't. I just couldn't. I tried to tell him, but he wouldn't listen. If I had stopped, if I had just stepped away and broken that sequence... I don't know, it just felt like the whole bay, the whole station, would just go bad. Like something terrible would happen if the sequence wasn't finished. So I wouldn't move. And now... now I'm here. Because he didn't understand the pattern.\n\nDoctor: When you're not in the middle of doing the pattern, do you ever think to yourself that the fear something bad will happen if you don't finish it might be excessive or not really true, or do you feel certain that it's a real danger no matter what?\n\nPatient: Excessive? No, I... why would I think it's excessive? That doesn't make any sense. It's not a... it's not some imagination thing, Dr. Smith. It's a real, physical certainty. Even when I'm not right there in the bay, even if I'm just sitting in the break room drinking coffee, or driving my car, I know exactly what the stakes are. \n\nBecause you have to understand how the dispatch system works. The numbers they give us, they aren't just random digits, they are tied to the calls, to the reality of what's happening out on the streets. And the equipment in the rig—the O2, the airway bags, the trauma dressings—they have to match that reality. So if I don't finish the pattern, the alignment is off. It's like... it's like leaving a door wide open in a hurricane. You wouldn't sit there in your living room while the wind is tearing your roof off and think, \"Gee, maybe my fear of the storm is excessive.\" You'd know the danger is right there. It's undeniable. \n\nIt's the exact same thing with the rigs. The danger is absolutely real, one hundred percent, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. If the sequence is broken, the bad thing is already happening. It's already bleeding into the station. That's why I couldn't step away when the lieutenant told me to. He was basically asking me to just let the hurricane in. He just sees a guy moving bags around, he doesn't see how the numbers lock it all down and keep us safe. So, no, I never doubt it. I never think it's not true. I honestly just sit here worrying that because he made me stop, because I'm here in this office right now instead of finishing the sequence, something terrible is probably happening to my crew on shift today.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this concern about the equipment patterns and numbers, have you noticed anything else unusual lately—like hearing things other people don't hear, or feeling that other unrelated events are also connected or significant in a special way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't been hearing voices or anything like that, if that's what you mean. The station is loud enough as it is, you know, with the tones dropping and the radios constantly squawking. I don't need to hear things that aren't there. But... well, since you mention other things being connected, there is this whole other situation going on at the station. And it's not just a feeling, it's something I know for a fact is happening, even though they try to play it off as nothing.\n\nSee, we have these cameras in the bays and around the station. Supposedly for security, right? To watch the rigs, make sure nobody wanders in off the street and messes with the narcotics box or steals our gear. That's what they tell us. But I know for a fact that the injury review people—the ones downtown in the admin building—and some of my own supervisors are using those cameras to watch me. Specifically me. They sit there and monitor what I'm doing, how I'm moving the bags, how I'm running my checks. \n\nAnd it doesn't stop there. It's the paperwork, too. Every time we run a call, we have to fill out these electronic patient care reports, the ePCRs. You have to document everything—the vitals, the interventions, the triage notes. It's a whole process, and I'm very meticulous about it. I have to be. But lately, I've been going back into the system to check my reports, and things are changed. My triage notes, the narratives I write... they're altered. Just slightly, but enough to make it look like I'm making mistakes. Like I'm being careless or unsafe on scene. \n\nI even confronted one of the supervisors about it. I brought him the printouts, showed him the discrepancies from what I originally typed. And you know what he said? He just brushed it off. He told me it was just \"routine paperwork audits\" and that the system sometimes auto-corrects or updates formatting. Formatting! You don't auto-correct a GCS score or a trauma criteria check! They're doing it on purpose, Dr. Smith. They're watching me through those lenses, tracking my movements, and then going into the secure servers to change my notes. They want to build a case against me to prove I'm unsafe, probably because they know I understand the patterns and they don't. They want me out of the way before I can get the rigs properly aligned.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several weeks, have you also noticed any prolonged periods of feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or low on energy, or conversely periods of feeling extremely elated, energetic, or needing little sleep?\n\nPatient: Sad? Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. \n\nAnd as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. \n\nAnd on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\nDoctor: How long has this specific concern about the equipment patterns, dispatch numbers, and being watched/altered records been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: Months. Definitely months. Well, about two months, to be exact. I mean, it is not like they just flipped a switch overnight, you know? But it was right around... yeah, about two months ago. That is when I first started really piecing it all together and seeing the scope of what they were doing. \n\nI remember because that is when I first noticed the lens on the main bay camera physically tracking me. The little indicator light would stay on way longer than it was supposed to after I walked past the rigs, just watching me arrange the trauma bags. And the paperwork stuff, the ePCRs... that started right around the exact same time. I can trace it back to this one specific shift about eight weeks ago. We had a really heavy rotation, and I spent an extra forty-five minutes at the end of my shift making sure every single triage note and trauma criteria check was perfectly, absolutely perfectly documented. And then a few days later, I go back into the secure server, and the formatting is changed. The notes are shifted just enough to look sloppy. \n\nSo, yeah, the camera and the paperwork stuff has been going on for about the last two months. And of course, that is exactly when the rest of the station started getting all worked up about me. The lieutenant pulling me into the office, the whispers in the day room... all that station behavior that got everyone so alarmed. They act like I am the one doing something wrong, getting all panicked and alarmed just because I am taking the time to make sure the equipment is properly aligned with the dispatch codes. \n\nTwo solid months of me just trying to do my job and keep the crew safe, and two months of them watching my every move, changing my official medical notes, and acting like I am the problem. It is just... it takes a massive amount of focus to deal with that kind of sabotage every single shift for eight straight weeks, Dr. Smith. If they would just open their eyes and look at how the numbers correlate to the streets, they would understand, but instead they have just spent the last two months trying to build this ridiculous case to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these concerns about the equipment, cameras, and paperwork, how have things been going in other areas of your life lately—like your relationships with family or friends, your ability to relax or enjoy hobbies, and your day-to-day self-care?\n\nPatient: Well, outside of the station... there isn't really much of an 'outside' anymore, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. I mean, when my shift ends and I finally leave that environment, I go back to my apartment and... I just sit there. \n\nIt's hard to explain, but it's like the second I walk through my front door, everything just stops. Most days I just sit in my apartment, usually right on the edge of the couch, sometimes without even taking my boots off right away. I don't turn on the TV, I don't put on any music. I just sit. I look over, and there's just trash piling up in the corner of the room. I can see it, I know I need to gather it up and walk it down to the dumpster behind the complex, but I just... I don't. I just stare at it. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my kitchen. I used to be the guy who cooked all the time, you know? You have to stay healthy in EMS, so I\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months when the camera and paperwork concerns became clear, did you notice any earlier changes—even subtle ones, like being more withdrawn, less motivated, or preoccupied with rules and patterns—that started earlier, say three to six months ago?\n\nPatient: Well, if we're really going back... if we're really looking at the timeline of when things started shifting, it wasn't just out of nowhere. It actually goes back a lot further than two months. It started... gosh, it must have been about eight months ago. Yeah, eight months. \n\nSee, there was this transfer we were doing. It was supposed to be a routine bariatric transport, nothing we haven't done a hundred times, but the gurney caught on the threshold of the ER bay doors. It just jammed, and the whole weight shifted, and I had to throw my entire body weight into it to keep the patient from going over. I ended up wrenching my shoulder pretty bad, and I cracked a couple of ribs on the side rail. \n\nIt was excruciating, Dr. Smith. Just breathing hurt. I had to take time off, obviously, because you can't lift a backboard with busted ribs and a torn rotator cuff. And being stuck at home... that's when things started getting quiet. Too quiet. I was just sitting there, day after day, waiting to heal, and I started having all this time to think. I wasn't out on the rigs, I wasn't running calls, I was just... detached. \n\nI guess you could say I was withdrawn, but it wasn't intentional, it was just that I couldn't physically do anything. And when I finally did get cleared for light duty and then back to full duty, I just... I wasn't the same. I couldn't just brush off the sloppy work anymore. I started noticing how careless everyone was being with the equipment, how the protocols weren't being followed to the letter. I started realizing that if I didn't pay attention to the patterns, if I didn't make sure the rules were strictly enforced, someone else was going to get hurt, or worse. \n\nSo, yes, the intense focus on the patterns, the need to have everything perfectly aligned... that really started building up after the injury, about eight months ago. I had to make sure the environment was controlled, because I learned the hard way what happens when things slip out of control. It just took a while for it to escalate to what's happening now with the cameras and the records, but the foundation of it, the real beginning... that was the shoulder and rib injury. That's when I really started to see how fragile the whole system is.\n\nDoctor: Since that injury about eight months ago, has this withdrawal and difficulty keeping up with things like cleaning or cooking been going on continuously the whole time, or were there stretches where you felt back to your normal self before the camera and paperwork concerns started two months ago?\n\nPatient: No... no, there weren't any stretches where I felt back to normal. Not really. It's been pretty much a continuous, straight line ever since I got hurt eight months ago. I mean, I kept thinking maybe once the ribs healed and the shoulder stopped throbbing I’d just naturally bounce back to my old routine, but... well, like I was trying to explain before, most days I just sit in my apartment. I literally just sit there. And it's been exactly like that the whole time. \n\nI just sit there with the trash piling up in the corner, and... and the kitchen, right, I was going to tell you about the kitchen earlier. The food is still in the fridge. I’ll go to the grocery store on auto-pilot, I’ll buy chicken and vegetables and rice because you have to eat right to do this job, but then I just put it away and leave it in the drawers until it rots. I don't cook like I used to. I used to meal prep every single Sunday, I had all these glass containers perfectly stacked and portioned out for the week... but now, I just can't get myself to start anything. It's too much. \n\nAnd it’s not just the cooking or the apartment. It’s everything else, too. I used to play pickup basketball with some of the guys from the other stations. Tuesday and Thursday mornings, without fail, we’d be out there at the community center running full-court games. I was always the one organizing it, sending the group texts, making sure we had enough guys for five-on-five. But since the injury? I haven't called the guys for basketball once. Not a single time in eight solid months. I see their names in my phone, I know I should probably reach out or at least go shoot around, but... I just can't get myself to care. I really can't. \n\nIt's like the starter motor in my head is just totally disconnected. I sit there on the edge of the couch, I look at the trash bags in the corner, I know the food is going bad in the fridge, I know the guys are probably playing without me, and I just... I don't care. I can't make myself care enough to just stand up and do any of it. So, yeah, to answer your question, it hasn't stopped or gotten better. It's just been this continuous, heavy feeling of not being able to start anything, just sitting there day after day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. {"candidates": ["F20.9", "F20.81", "F22"], "note": "Duration (8 months) with prodromal negative symptoms (avolition, social withdrawal, anhedonia, self-care decline) followed by delusions of persecution/reference exceeds 6 months, supporting F20.9 over F20.81. Fixed, non-bizarre-leaning but systematized persecutory delusions (cameras, altered records) alongside bizarre magical-thinking beliefs (numeric alignment preventing disaster) keep both F20.9 and F22 viable; mood symptoms explicitly denied, ruling out mood-related psychotic disorders.", "is_final": false}

It's like... it's like leaving a door wide open in a hurricane. It's undeniable. \n\nIt's the exact same thing with the rigs. The danger is absolutely real, one hundred percent, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. He was basically asking me to just let the hurricane in. He just sees a guy moving bags around, he doesn't see how the numbers lock it all down and keep us safe. So, no, I never doubt it. I never think it's not true. I honestly just sit here worrying that because he made me stop, because I'm here in this office right now instead of finishing the sequence, something terrible is probably happening to my crew on shift today.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this concern about the equipment patterns and numbers, have you noticed anything else unusual lately—like hearing things other people don't hear, or feeling that other unrelated events are also connected or significant in a special way?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't been hearing voices or anything like that, if that's what you mean. You don't auto-correct a GCS score or a trauma criteria check! They're doing it on purpose, Dr. Smith. They're watching me through those lenses, tracking my movements, and then going into the secure servers to change my notes. They want me out of the way before I can get the rigs properly aligned.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several weeks, have you also noticed any prolonged periods of feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or low on energy, or conversely periods of feeling extremely elated, energetic, or needing little sleep?\n\nPatient: Sad? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nSad? Hopeless? No, Dr. Smith, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, sure, it's frustrating, right? Anyone would be frustrated if they were being watched constantly and their official medical notes were being tampered with. But sad? No. I don't have the luxury of sitting around feeling sorry for myself or being hopeless. If I give up, who is going to fix the sequence? Nobody. The rest of the crew sure isn't going to do it, they don't even see it. \n\nAnd as for energy... look, when you're working a 24-hour shift on a busy rig, your energy is going to fluctuate. That's just basic biology. You get tones dropping at 3:00 AM for a motor vehicle collision or a respiratory distress call, you have to be awake. You go from zero to sixty. So yeah, sometimes I'm tired after a shift, but not... not like some unnatural low energy where I can't function. I have to function. The rigs have to be checked. \n\nAnd on the flip side, I'm not bouncing off the walls or staying awake for days on end just for the fun of it. I sleep when I can, when the radio is quiet. If I seem really focused or driven right now, it's just because I have a massive amount of work to do to keep things aligned, especially with administration breathing down my neck and trying to build this fake case against me. You have to be hyper-vigilant when the stakes are this high. It takes a lot of mental stamina to keep the dispatch numbers straight in your head while you're physically arranging the trauma bags, but that's not... I'm not overly elated or whatever you called it. I'm just doing my job. \n\nHonestly, the only thing that brings me down even a little bit is just the sheer incompetence of it all. You know? Like, I'm trying to protect the crew, I'm trying to make sure the oxygen and the airway bags are perfectly synchronized with the reality of the streets, and my lieutenant is just completely oblivious. He's telling me to step away. But that's not depression, Dr. Smith. That's just the reality of dealing with people who refuse to open their eyes. So, no, my mood is completely normal. I'm just very, very busy trying to hold the station together before they manage to ruin everything.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. It just jammed, and the whole weight shifted, and I had to throw my entire body weight into it to keep the patient from going over. I ended up wrenching my shoulder pretty bad, and I cracked a couple of ribs on the side rail. \n\nIt was excruciating, Dr. Smith. Just breathing hurt. I had to take time off, obviously, because you can't lift a backboard with busted ribs and a torn rotator cuff. And being stuck at home... that's when things started getting quiet. Too quiet. I was just sitting there, day after day, waiting to heal, and I started having all this time to think. I wasn't out on the rigs, I wasn't running calls, I was just... detached. \n\nI guess you could say I was withdrawn, but it wasn't intentional, it was just that I couldn't physically do anything. And when I finally did get cleared for light duty and then back to full duty, I just... I wasn't the same. I couldn't just brush off the sloppy work anymore. I started noticing how careless everyone was being with the equipment, how the protocols weren't being followed to the letter. I started realizing that if I didn't pay attention to the patterns, if I didn't make sure the rules were strictly enforced, someone else was going to get hurt, or worse. \n\nSo, yes, the intense focus on the patterns, the need to have everything perfectly aligned... that really started building up after the injury, about eight months ago. I had to make sure the environment was controlled, because I learned the hard way what happens when things slip out of control.
